@@138594041 <h> Ireland 's EU Presidency : What it is and why it matters <p> The eyes of Europe will fall on Ireland today as the State assumes the Presidency of the European Union . <p> It will be the eighth time Ireland has taken on the role since we joined the EEC in 1973 . <p> However , compared to previous presidencies , given the cycle of crises the EU has confronted in recent years , the stakes could not be higher : The sheer volume of legislation that the Government will be expected to steer through the EU 's law-making machinery could not be greater . <p> In the first of two articles RT ? ' s Europe Editor Tony Connelly explains the rotating role of the EU presidency , how Ireland has prepared for it and what challenges the State will face . <p> Back in 2013 , the EU Presidency fell on a country still badly bruised by the banking crisis and subsequent EU/IMF bailout . <p> The spend was around ? 64 million , just over half the amount spent in 2004 ( diplomats called it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2013 , Ireland has been a net contributor to the EU and the country is one of the wealthiest member states according to GDP - the Presidency today will cost anything up to ? 400m , once security costs have been taken into account . <p> With the sequence of crises that has shaken the EU - from terrorism , migration , and Brexit to Covid , Russia 's invasion of Ukraine and the return of Donald Trump - member states have granted the EU deeper and more far-reaching powers . <p> " The EU is a much bigger beast than it was in 2013 " , says one Irish diplomat . " It 's a different world . There are more competencies and they are more complicated . " <p> Furthermore , the credit shown to Ireland for its recovery from the banking crisis , perhaps reflected in the unwavering support shown by the EU during the Brexit negotiations , has been tempered by a more critical view of the country 's reliance of US tech corporations and the critique that it has not paid its way in terms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ maritime border . <p> For this and other reasons , the stakes for the next six months could not be higher . <p> Anglo Irish Bank - Nationalised in 2009 <p> But what exactly is the Presidency for ? <p> In Ireland , there will be the operational challenge of hosting hundreds of meetings ; in Brussels , Ireland will be expected to negotiate and close EU legislation . <p> The Government has set out four objectives : <p> Running a successful Presidency in Brussels : EU member states and the European Parliament are mainly co-legislators , so when the European Commission proposes legislation Ireland 's role will be to negotiate a common position among 27 member states . <p> Running a successful operation in Ireland : Hosting 280 meetings is an opportunity to showcase the country 's soft power and positive attitude to the EU and foreign visitors . <p> Asserting the Government 's policy priorities : This is about taking the current EU agenda and steering it in a direction the Government thinks will best suit member states ( including Ireland ) and the European project . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is about more than fleets of State cars sweeping in and out of Dublin Castle , that there is " buy in " from communities across the country . <p> While support for the EU remains at 82% - unchanged from 2025 - the latest European Movement Ireland ( EMI ) poll suggests the bloc 's reputation has taken a knock over its handling of the Israel-Gaza War : Less than half of the population ( 45% ) believe the EU is headed in the right direction and 33% believe it is not upholding its core values . <p> " The Irish Government sees this as an opportunity to build and reconnect the EU with the Irish citizen , " Aingeal O'Donoghue , Ireland 's ambassador to the EU , recently told an event at the European Policy Centre ( EPC ) , " even though Ireland is and remains one of the most pro-European countries " . <p> Key departments - especially Foreign Affairs , DETE , Environment and Transport - will be consumed by the legislative responsibilities in Brussels . <p> The Department of Foreign Affairs ( DFA @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ departments to assess the spectrum of the EU 's legislative programme for the next six months . <p> Some legislative files being steered by Irish officials in Brussels will require a lot of input from ministers . <p> Some legislative files being steered by Irish officials in Brussels will require input from ministers <p> Taoiseach Miche ? l Martin , with a longer political memory than most , has referred privately to the Fine Gael/Labour coalition taking their eye off the domestic ball during the 1996 EU presidency , allowing Fianna F ? il to win the general election in 1997 . <p> " We will have a big program of meetings in Ireland and we need to not mess that up , " says one Dublin source . " You want everybody to come and go back at least with no bad memories , ideally with good memories . You want the media to be fed , you want the security to work . " <p> The latter has been a concern , with the risk of drone incursions high enough to prompt the Government to spend ? 19m @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Government wants a repeat of the fuel protests that brought the country to a standstill in April . <p> Protesters blocked O'Connell Street in Dublin during demonstrations in April <p> Compared to 2013 , there is more than double the number of meetings planned in Ireland - some 280 in total . Around three quarters will happen in Dublin , the rest scattered across the country . <p> There will be 22 informal gatherings of EU ministers , but the biggest undertaking will be the European Political Community ( EPC ) - the biannual gathering of continental EU and non-EU leaders - which will be followed by an informal European Council . <p> Both meetings will take place in Dublin on 12 and 13 November and will pose the biggest diplomatic and security challenge the State has faced to date . <p> The 22 ministerial gatherings , known as Informal Councils , will cover everything from foreign affairs and defence ( in Druid 's Glen on 1-2 September ) , to agriculture , fisheries , finance , environment , housing and international development . <p> These meetings tend to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out again . <p> The hundreds of fringe meetings , by contrast , which will bring in thousands of delegates , will be looser , more relaxed affairs where Irish music and hospitality can be laid on a little more thickly . <p> To boost the effort , the Government decided that each of the 26 Irish counties ( south of the border ) will be paired with a member state . <p> Limerick has been paired with Germany , for example , meaning a programme of events including international conferences hosted by Mary Immaculate College , the University of Limerick and the Technological University of the Shannon bringing together academics , researchers and policymakers from Ireland , Germany and across Europe . <p> Limerick has been paired with Germany <p> What is the role of the Presidency in Brussels ? <p> Since the Lisbon Treaty , the European Council - ie , the forum which brings together 27 prime ministers - has been chaired by a permanent president , in this case Ant ? nio Costa . Meetings of foreign affairs ministers are chaired by the EU 's foreign @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policy areas , Ireland chairs all formations that bring together 27 national governments . Its role is to forge a common position on legislative proposals . <p> Once that position has been adopted , Ireland has a mandate from 26 governments to negotiate with the European Parliament to turn the legislative files into EU law . <p> That is done through a so-called Trilogue , the most intense part of the process . <p> On most occasions , the Irish Presidency will be represented in a trilogue by either of the two Irish ambassadors to the EU ( Aingeal O'Donoghue or C ? it Moran ) ; the Parliament will be represented by the rapporteur from the relevant committee , and the European Commission will be present as an observer and guide . <p> " The trilogues are a bit of a black box for the Council ie , the member states " , says one EU diplomat . " You send the Presidency into the trilogue , you give them a mandate , you send them to negotiate , they come back and tell you what happened . But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ know what 's happening there . " <p> There is an inherent tension : The European Parliament tends to want more out of the legislation , while the member states want less ( Ireland is there on behalf of the member states ) . <p> Trilogues have also become a more bruising confrontation . The European Parliament is more assertive about its role , and the rise in far-right parties has made the process less predictable . <p> " The Parliament has become ever better in defending its prerogatives " , says Sabina Lange , associate professor , Center for Diplomatic , Economic and Legal studies at the University of Ljubljana , and director of Think Europe . <p> " On the other hand , it has become ever more difficult to actually understand what the Parliament 's priority or majority is because of the positions of the political groups , because often there are alternative majorities available . " <p> The Parliament 's negotiators also know that a Presidency is only there for six months , meaning they can pressurise Irish officials to cut a deal ( MEPs , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) . <p> That has led to recent situations where a trilogue deal goes back to the member states in Coreper and it is promptly rejected or questioned . <p> The trilogue is also where the political aims of the legislation collide with the technical aspects of how to implement that legislation out across 27 member states . <p> If there is no breakthrough , the Presidency will propose a breakout meeting , where a handful of key negotiators will get into a separate room to thrash out a compromise . <p> Getting to the trilogue is , in itself , an arduous journey . <p> New proposals from the European Commission go both to the relevant European Parliament committee(s) and also to the so-called Working Parties of the Council , i.e. civil servants and diplomats from the 27 member states . <p> European Union flags fly at the European Commission <p> All sides start pouring over the proposal to see where it may conflict with national sensitivities or political imperatives . <p> Each Working Party will be chaired by an Irish official . <p> " It 's not just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , " but there 's also a team around the chair who are going to run each Working Party , who are preparing for each meeting , figuring out what is the position of all 26 of the member states . <p> " How am I going to run the meeting ? What papers do I need ? Who do I need to talk to in advance ? " <p> Once the legislation has been put through the Working Party process , with each member state suggesting amendments , the Irish chair will have to decide if there is sufficient consensus to push the file up to the next level : Coreper , or the Committee of Permanent Representatives . <p> Coreper is made up of 27 national ambassadors to the EU , all doing the bidding of their governments . <p> Much of the EU 's legislation is agreed through a weighted consensus known as a Qualified Majority Vote ( QMV ) . A QMV vote is carried if 16 out 27 member states agree , and those member states represent at least 65% of the EU population . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ know when the file is ready to send up to Coreper so that it gains at least a QMV level of support . If the file arrives and is promptly rejected , that will be a waste of everybody 's time and a black mark against the Presidency . <p> The Government has put huge resources into boosting staff in Ireland 's Permanent Representation to the EU in Brussels ( known as the Perm Rep ) . The personnel has more than doubled to 220 . <p> Several thousand officials have been trained in Dublin and Brussels to cope with over 170 meetings of Working Parties , as well as all the other meetings . <p> If the legislative file is agreed at Coreper , then it goes further up the chain to the Council of Ministers ( eg , Ecofin , Agri-Fish , Environment ) for further political assessment . <p> At a certain point , the file is either blocked , because one or other member state is getting cold feet ( in which case the issue may go up to the next summit of EU leaders ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is what the Irish Presidency then takes to the trilogue . <p> Two years in advance of the Presidency , Irish ambassadors were tasked with getting to know national and regional politicians in every EU member state ( even within the EU 's legislative machinery , all politics is local ) . <p> Irish ministers and diplomats have been attending every European Parliament plenary session in Strasbourg to glad-hand members of the most important committees . <p> Thomas Byrne , Minister of State for European Affairs , recently told a public meeting in Brussels : " If you 're going into trilogues , if you 're going to pass EU laws , you have to know who your counterparts are . <p> " Putting that emphasis on the European Parliament in the past year I hope will have paid off , because we know these people well , we 're getting to know them personally . " <p> Minister of State for European Affairs Thomas Byrne <p> But all that preparation is often no match for the curved balls that the current geopolitical climate tends to throw . It will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Council or Coreper meetings . <p> The Danish EU presidency in 2025 had scheduled 25 Coreper meetings . In the event , there were 55 meetings as the EU struggled to respond to the return of Donald Trump and his tariff war . <p> Many took place in the " bunker " , two floors below ground in the Council headquarters in Brussels , where phones are left at the door . <p> When a hastily arranged summit between the EU and President Trump took place in Turnberry , Scotland in July , the 27 EU ambassadors of Coreper were in Nuuk , Greenland on an away day . <p> They were all bustled into a hotel conference room to give guidance to the European Commission team by videolink . <p> " In the current geopolitical context you need to be able to adapt and to improvise " , Carsten Gr ? nbech-Jensen , Denmark 's ambassador to the EU , told RT ? News . <p> " But it 's much better if you have a solid plan to work from . We felt the difference from 2012 where @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ faced multiple urgencies at the same time across policy areas . That 's the new reality for a Council Presidency . <p> " In 2025 the whole international background was a lot more volatile : Ukraine - a real war in Europe , and lots of related defence and security challenges , the new American president , who wanted to change our trading relationship and introduce tariffs , pressure to advance quickly on several policy files to strengthen European competitiveness in response to the Draghi report , migration reform . We had to adapt a lot more . " <p> Which begs the question : How far can Ireland advance its own agenda while managing a vast panoply of negotiations and geopolitical shocks ? <p> Throughout the Presidency , Ireland is expected to be a neutral chair . However , with skill , officials can schedule meetings and set agendas to advance the State 's priorities , or at least to not waste meetings and interpreter time on policy files which are n't getting anywhere . <p> Ahead of the Danish presidency , prime minister Mette Frederiksen poured cold water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the legislative process needed political leadership . <p> As such , Denmark championed those files that were in tune with its national priorities ( climate goals , migration , security and defence , and the support of Ukraine ) , carefully aligning its position with the European Commission on key areas before the Presidency started , and being a tough chair in trilogue negotiations on some legislative files that were important to Copenhagen . <p> Ireland has an excellent reputation in Brussels based on previous presidencies , and member states are expecting a professional operation this time around . <p> " Irish officials give the impression that they 're well prepared , they 've been doing the work , they 've been putting the right people in place , " says one EU diplomat . " The expectations in that sense are reasonably high . " <p> There is also the risk that Irish ministers will be pressured by bigger member states to move files in a certain direction . <p> A number of sources have suggested that during the Irish Presidency in 2013 , the then German Chancellor Angela @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a regulation on diesel emissions - that were later implicated in the Volkswagen Dieselgate scandal - from the agenda of a Coreper meeting . <p> There are plenty of policy areas this time around where France and Germany have strong preferences . <p> Despite that , Irish sources are clear that they will mount a professional presidency . <p> One diplomat remarked : " Sometimes a large country presidency thinks they 're going to remake the EU in their name . <p> " We 're not going to do that . But within the context of what a presidency can do , and within the challenges that the world has , we want to make use of the opportunity to move the EU in the direction we think it needs to be moved in . " <p> Tomorrow , Tony Connelly will look at the EU policies the Irish Government will be expected to steer towards a conclusion during its six month Presidency .

The vote is likely to be held via secret ballot . The motion has been put forward to members by the FAI themselves , and is not a vote on whether to boycott the fixtures . The motion instead calls for members to endorse the fulfilling of the fixtures , and makes no explicit mention of Israel . <p> It reads as follows : " While acknowledging the strength of feeling regarding support for Palestine and the upcoming @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recognise the profound impact that any non-fulfilment of UEFA fixtures would have on Irish football as a whole and on its future development , and accordingly endorses the Association fulfilling its obligations in respect of those fixtures . " <p> Members of the FAI general assembly had previously submitted a motion for a boycott of the Israel fixtures , but the FAI ruled this inadmissible under the Association 's rules as it was not supported by the mandated 10 per cent of the Association 's membership . <p> The FAI board have explained their reasoning for fulfilling the fixtures in a seven-page letter to members calling for support of the motion , which has been seen by The Irish Times . FAI president Paul Cooke and chairman Tony Keohane write that the board concluded that boycotting the fixtures would " cause significant and lasting harm to Irish football " . <p> A view of tennis balls thrown on the pitch in protest by fans during the friendly international against Qatar at the Aviva Stadium in May . Photograph : Laszlo Geczo/Inpho <p> The letter lists some of the potential sanctions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ boycott the games . They repeat some of the known potential consequences , including the forfeiting of games and potential relegation to League C of the Nations League and the consequent loss of Fifa ranking points . To this they have added the potential exclusion from the next edition of the Nations League for reasons of " competitive integrity " , and the potential exclusion from future Uefa and Fifa competitions at senior and underage level . <p> In this letter , the FAI enumerate for the first time the potential cost of a boycott , estimating it at a minimum of ? 5.5 million . They say this is the direct potential impact , consisting of the loss of Uefa participation income , ticketing , revenue , and compensation . The FAI say the likely costs could climb to ? 10.3 million when the impact relegation would have on ticketing and sponsorship is priced in , and say there could be further , significant impact due to the " possible removal from Uefa competitions " . <p> The FAI say that a boycott would set a precedent whereby all @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ forced to confront " difficult decisions with financial and sporting impacts " . They also point to their co-hosting of Euro 2028 , saying that " maintaining a positive and constructive relationship with Uefa is essential " for the delivery of the tournament , infrastructure and development funding , future hosting opportunities , and Ireland 's standing within European football . The FAI state that they would have no authority to exclude Israel from playing Euro 2028 games in Dublin should they qualify , which , in the FAI 's words , " makes a boycott in the short-term both inconsistent and ineffective " . <p> The FAI 's letter argues that a boycott would not punish Israel , but punish Irish football , and say they will continue to engage with Uefa regarding Israel 's participation and explore appropriate support for civilians affected by the humanitarian crisis in Gaza . <p> Listen to America 2026 our special World Cup podcast with Kevin Kilbane and Paul Howard <p> Sign up for push alerts to get the best breaking news , analysis and comment delivered to your phone

@@138626071 <h> Exhibition explores the lived experience of Gaza <p> University of Galway is hosting a four-day exhibition exploring daily life in Gaza through personal testimony , photography , video , letters and children 's artwork . <p> The exhibition centres on the experiences of Palestinian entrepreneur , writer and public speaker Shirene Yaseen , whose family remains in Gaza . <p> The Lived Experience of Gaza opened Wednesday July 1 , and will run until to Saturday July 4 from 9am to 6pm in An D ? nlann , the University of Galway Art Gallery in the Quadrangle . <p> Shirene Yaseen will travel from her base in Doha , Qatar , to open the exhibition and deliver a special address in the Michael D Higgins Auditorium in the Quadrangle of the University on Friday July 3 at 10am , sharing her family 's experience of survival and displacement . <p> The event will be followed by a peace panel exploring what institutional protection means for human dignity and what its absence costs . <p> Through personal accounts and visual materials , the exhibition documents the realities of displacement , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ facing families living amid the ongoing conflict and humanitarian crisis . <p> It asks whether the silence since the ceasefire reflects the actual situation in Palestine , or whether the world has simply stopped paying attention . <p> This event is part of the Thinking Beyond thought leadership series hosted by the J.E . Cairnes School of Business and Economics at University of Galway . <p> Admission is free and registration is available online . Funding for the project was secured through the University of Galway Sustainability Engagement Fund . <p> Shirene was five years old in 2000 when she first witnessed someone being killed . She has lived through more than seven wars since . Her story is not one that began in October 2023 , it is the story of a lifetime . <p> Since the most recent conflict began , Shirene 's family have been displaced nearly 20 times . They continue to live without functioning healthcare , schools , safety , and certainty of where they will sleep . Shirene left Gaza and can not go back , and her family can not leave . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but the world has moved on . I carry a deep helplessness because I left and they can not . I want people to understand that this is not over . My family is fighting to survive every day , and they deserve to be seen . " <p> Sophie Sweeney , Lecturer at the University of Galway and organiser of the exhibition , said , " There is a world of difference between following a conflict in the news and hearing directly from someone living it . " <p> " When I met Shirene , I knew this story had to come to Ireland . We have a long tradition of standing with displaced communities , and this felt like something our university and city should be part of . " <p> Among the stories featured in the exhibition is that of Shirene 's sister , who gave birth on a staircase in the dark without medical assistance . @@138647272 <h> Stripping it back <p> Gilbert O'Sullivan told David Hennessy about his new live record , feeling at home in Ireland and avoiding the red carpet . <p> With his new live album Songbook , Gilbert O'Sullivan reimagines eleven well known songs from his 57 year career . <p> O'Sullivan 's songwriting has earned him top 10 singles and No. 1s around the world , including the classic Alone Again ( Naturally ) which topped the U.S. charts for six weeks and earned him three Grammy nominations . <p> British recognition soon followed with the songs Clair and Get Down reaching the summit of the U.K. singles charts and his LP Back to Front topping the album charts . <p> Gilbert O'Sullivan has headline Glastonbury , been nominated for three Grammy Awards and won three Ivor Novellos including Songwriter of the Year in 1973 . <p> But it should come as no surprise that it was not Gilbert O'Sullivan 's idea in the beginning as he is never one for looking back and is always keener to look ahead to the next album . <p> The album he expects to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ career will also reach six decades in 2027 but Gilbert does n't get carried away with anniversaries or milestones either . <p> However what he is proudest of is to still be doing almost 60 years on from starting out . <p> - Advertisement - <p> The idea for Songbook came after the acclaim and success of his last two studio albums . <p> BMG came up with the idea of recording stripped back versions of hits like Clair , Alone Again ( Naturally ) and Nothing Rhymed to capture the magic of the live shows . <p> O'Sullivan insisted the songs should be recorded at a venue with no audience and not in a studio . @@138655865 <p> From the trenches of Ukraine to the Iranian ballistic missile threat , NATO 's security environment is becoming increasingly multi-theatre -- and increasingly unpredictable . When allied leaders meet in Ankara on July 7 -- 8 , they will do so against a backdrop of overlapping wars and widening geopolitical fault lines that are reshaping the transatlantic alliance 's strategic priorities faster than formal doctrine can keep pace . <p> The summit comes as Russia 's war in Ukraine grinds into its fourth year , the recent US-Israeli military conflict with Iran has heightened instability across the Middle East , and allies continue grappling with growing demands for higher defence spending amid economic pressures at home . <p> Unlike previous summits that focused on enlarging the alliance or redefining NATO 's strategic vision , the Ankara meeting is expected to centre on implementation -- translating ambitious political commitments into military capability . <p> That includes expanding defence industrial production , strengthening NATO 's deterrence posture , delivering long-term military support for Ukraine and responding to a rapidly evolving security landscape stretching from Eastern Europe to the Middle East . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Erdogan said Ankara would provide the " strongest platform " for allies to exchange experience while calling for an " unconditional " security architecture capable of responding to increasingly complex global threats . <p> During a phone call with German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Monday , Erdogan also underlined the need for a strong commitment from allies to boost Europe 's defence and preserve the " transatlantic bond " as NATO enters what many describe as a new strategic phase . <p> European Union foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas , who visited T ? rkiye ahead of the summit , described the Ankara gathering as a " historic " moment for the alliance , arguing that NATO must project unity at a time when transatlantic relations are under strain . She said the summit would focus on increasing defence production , strengthening deterrence and assessing " what more can we do for Ukraine , " while urging greater investment in joint military capabilities . <h> From strategy to implementation <p> For Mehmet Ozkan , Professor of International Relations at the Joint War Institute of T ? rkiye 's National Defence @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ NATO 's evolution . <p> " The Ankara Summit should primarily be understood as an implementation summit rather than a forum for major strategic recalibration , " he told TRT World . <p> " The central issue is no longer defining a new threat or revising NATO 's strategic concept . It is operationalising commitments on defence spending , industrial production and military capability development . " <p> According to Ozkan , the alliance is entering what he describes as its second post-Cold War transformation , shifting from defining itself through external threats toward building a sustainable defence-industrial base capable of supporting long-term deterrence . <p> Marian Duris , a foreign policy expert at the European Parliament in Brussels , broadly agrees that implementation will dominate the summit but argues that NATO can not avoid larger strategic questions . <p> " The significance of this event lies not in pompous statements but in recognising the current situation , the possible future and the ability to implement the respective positions , " he told TRT World . <p> " More pragmatism would be needed , less panic and a major strategic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ arguing that some policymakers remain trapped in geopolitical assumptions rooted in the late twentieth century while new security challenges continue to emerge . <h> Defence spending and industrial capacity <p> One of the summit 's principal objectives is expected to be implementing NATO 's ambitious new defence investment framework , under which allies have agreed to work towards investing 5 percent of GDP by 2035 , combining traditional defence expenditure with spending on infrastructure , resilience and military mobility . <p> Yet both experts argue that the alliance 's future effectiveness will depend less on financial commitments than on whether those investments produce tangible military capability . <p> " The sustainability of support for Ukraine will increasingly depend on industrial capacity rather than political declarations alone , " Ozkan said . <p> European governments face fiscal pressures , political constraints and growing public fatigue after years of supporting Kiev , he noted . NATO 's response has therefore been to institutionalise defence production and expand industrial output so that military assistance becomes sustainable over the long term . <p> Duris likewise cautions against reducing security solely to budgetary targets . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of defence spending , " he said . " It must reflect geography , industrial capacity and the identification of real threats . " <h> Ukraine remains NATO 's defining challenge <p> More than four years into Russia 's war in Ukraine , NATO leaders are expected to discuss how to sustain long-term military support for Kiev while addressing growing political and economic pressures across Europe . <p> Yet maintaining support for Ukraine is becoming politically more complex . Public fatigue , economic constraints and changing domestic politics across Europe have raised questions about whether governments can sustain military assistance indefinitely . <p> Ozkan argues that fiscal constraints , domestic political pressures and growing public fatigue across Europe make it increasingly important for NATO to institutionalise defence production rather than depend on ad hoc aid packages . <p> For Duris , however , domestic politics may increasingly constrain European governments . " The fatigue from the long-lasting war can be seen everywhere , " he said , noting that while many European institutions remain committed to supporting Ukraine , elected governments face growing domestic resistance as economic pressures mount . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is not whether NATO will continue supporting Ukraine , but whether the alliance can build the industrial and political capacity needed to sustain that support over the long term . <h> Iran puts NATO 's southern flank back in focus <p> If Ukraine remains NATO 's defining military challenge , the recent US-Israeli military campaign against Iran has expanded the alliance 's strategic agenda . While the Russia-Ukraine war continues to dominate NATO 's eastern flank , instability across the Middle East has reinforced concerns that threats on the southern flank can no longer be treated as secondary . <p> Although Iran lies outside NATO 's formal area of operations , the conflict has sharpened concerns that instability across the Middle East directly affects alliance security through energy markets , maritime trade routes , migration , cyber threats and the risk of wider regional escalation . <p> For T ? rkiye , which borders one of the world 's most volatile regions while maintaining dialogue with both Western allies and regional powers , the conflict has further underscored its strategic importance within NATO . <p> However , he believes these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " The summit 's defining theme will still be industrialisation and deterrence . The southern flank agenda will be discussed less as a separate regional issue and more as one of the drivers behind NATO 's need for greater resilience , readiness and defence production capacity . " <p> Duris similarly believes the confrontation with Iran has significantly reshaped NATO 's geopolitical calculations . " The broader situation in the Middle East is pushing some countries to pay greater attention to the southern flank because instability there has direct consequences through energy markets , maritime routes , escalation risks , migration pressures , cyber threats and wider strategic uncertainty , " he said . <p> Balancing these concerns with NATO 's continued focus on Russia and Ukraine will likely be one of the summit 's most delicate political challenges . <h> T ? rkiye 's growing recognition as an industrial power <p> President Erdogan has used the run-up to the summit to emphasise that alliance solidarity should include removing restrictions on defence trade among allies and integrating T ? rkiye more fully into European defence initiatives . <p> He @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ukraine to encompass instability in the Middle East , Gaza , Iran and broader challenges facing the alliance 's southern flank . <p> These priorities reflect Ankara 's broader ambition to position itself not only as NATO 's southeastern anchor , but also as an indispensable defence producer and strategic partner . <p> Duris sees the summit as recognition of T ? rkiye 's broader geopolitical significance . " T ? rkiye occupies a uniquely important geographic and political position at the intersection of Europe , the Black Sea region , the Mediterranean and the Middle East , " he said . <p> " It is not a coincidence . It is a conscious , long-term and careful strategy of the current Turkish leadership , combining multiple strategic vectors according to national interest . In the defence industry and technological field , this has brought tangible , rocket-like growth . " <p> EU 's Kallas also underscored T ? rkiye 's growing strategic importance , describing it as NATO 's second-largest military with a strong defence industry and " a very , very prominent role " in European security and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ essential given its influence across the Middle East , the Caucasus and the Black Sea region . <h> No crisis within NATO <p> Beyond external security threats , the Ankara Summit will also deliberate on the differences within NATO itself . <p> US President Donald Trump and his administration 's renewed emphasis on burden-sharing and a more transactional approach to alliances have revived debates over Europe 's strategic autonomy , while differing European responses to the recent US-Israeli war against Iran have exposed varying threat perceptions and national priorities within NATO . <p> According to Ozkan , maintaining consensus may now be NATO 's greatest challenge . " One of the key lessons from NATO 's post-Cold War experience is that the alliance 's greatest challenge is not always external threats , but the ability to maintain internal consensus on how to respond . " <p> He argues that NATO 's growing emphasis on defence-industrial cooperation is itself an effort to create a new basis for alliance cohesion rooted in shared economic and industrial interests rather than common threat perceptions alone . <p> Rather than aligning with competing camps , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pragmatic balancing actor capable of maintaining dialogue across different regions while emphasising alliance unity . <p> Duris likewise rejects suggestions that today 's disagreements are unprecedented . " Internal relations within NATO have never been entirely harmonious , " he noted . <p> While Trump 's rhetoric may appear more transactional than that of previous administrations , he argues the underlying differences ultimately reflect competing national interests rather than a fundamental crisis inside NATO . <p> " The Iranian conflict is the topic from which most tensions in NATO today stem , " he said . " Several European leaders are not enthusiastic about this conflict . It is ultimately about the interests of specific actors . " <p> The debate in Ankara , according to analysts , is therefore likely to extend beyond defence spending and military capabilities . It will also test whether NATO can maintain political cohesion as the alliance confronts simultaneous crises on its eastern and southern flanks , while accommodating increasingly diverse national interests and strategic priorities among its members .

@@138683365 <p> Irish America was at its high-water mark during the 1990s peace process , politically powerful at home and abroad . Today , as the United States marks its 250th anniversary , it still wields some power , but it is a shell of its former self . <p> With the anniversary of the signing of the US declaration of independence , positive references to Irish immigrants and their descendants abound , unlike other immigrant communities . <p> Irish Americans were not always celebrated in the US , but as they accumulated economic and political power , they became one of the most powerful ethnic lobbies in the US . <p> From this perch and on the back of often hard-won political power , they maintained strong relations with Ireland and ensured it had political access in Washington disproportionate to its size . <p> Today , however , the relationship between Ireland and the US is under profound stress , from both internal changes within Irish America and external pressures from Donald Trump and his Maga gadflies . <p> In the 1980 census , when Americans could only choose one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . In the 2020 census , when the rules changed to let people claim more than one country of ancestry , 38.6 million did . <p> In the intervening years , the US population increased by more than 100 million . Today , most Irish Americans are third or fourth generation immigrants with limited ties to their homeland . New blood is largely absent , bar a trickle of new immigrants from Ireland . <p> Irish Americans are increasingly disparate . They are no longer concentrated in cities like Boston , New York , Philadelphia or Chicago but are spread out across the US . Even though it is not realised much in Ireland , if at all , more identify as Protestant than Catholic . <p> And even though Irish Americans built their political power through the Democratic Party , they are now as politically split as the rest of the country . There is no longer a discernible Irish voting bloc . <p> Their political and cultural organisations have also waned . Membership in the Ancient Order of Hibernians has declined precipitously , and bitter debates about whether @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The Congressional Friends of Ireland Caucus has one-third the members it had in the 1990s and now competes for members with the newly formed Friends of the United Kingdom Caucus . <p> In the 2024 election cycle Irish America 's only political action committee , the Irish American Democrats , made just $20,000 ( ? 17,000 ) worth of contributions . By comparison , the American Israeli Public Affairs Committee contributed $52 million . Money talks . <p> Given its weaknesses , it is nothing short of astonishing that Irish America re-emerged to flex its muscles in the wake of Brexit , angered over the British government 's action that could have led to a new hard border on the island of Ireland . <p> The United States had no clear policy on Brexit , but Irish Americans , still including those who were tempered in the fire three decades ago , recognised the risk it posed to the peace process . <p> A group of politically well-connected figures including two former congressmen , Bruce Morrison and Jim Walsh , formed the Ad-Hoc Committee to Protect the Good @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ad Hoc committee lobbied congress to use the US-UK trade deal coveted by Brexiteers as leverage , tying the potential for a trade deal to the guarantee of an open border . <p> And it was not a one-off show of support . The Congressional Friends of Ireland and other Irish American groups kept pressure on the British government until the Windsor Agreement was signed in 2023 . <p> If and when it happens , Irish Americans will be energised by a unity referendum in Ireland . Some will offer full-throated support for it while others will focus on ensuring fair play in the referendum and afterwards . <p> But even the prospect of Irish unity can not transcend the demographic and other changes that undermine Irish American cohesion and potential for future collective action . <p> Though Ireland survived Trump 's first term largely unscathed , it faces a more belligerent Trump in the second . @@138820509 <p> The European Union could introduce a " partial or total " ban on trade coming from illegal Israeli settlements , or levy prohibitively high tariffs on imports , according to a confidential paper circulated to national governments . <p> The European Commission , the union 's powerful executive arm , has presented options to governments detailing how the EU could penalise Israel for the continuing expansion of settlements in the West Bank and other occupied Palestinian lands . <p> The internal paper , seen by The Irish Times , said options included an EU-wide ban on trade coming from illegal settlements , to levy steep tariffs on any goods sold into the European market , or set up a licensing system controlling and restricting imports . <p> The commission paper cautioned that EU action against Israeli settlements could end up " further exacerbating political extremist positions in Israel , " and create " hostility " towards Israel , or a " surge " in anti-Semitism . <p> Foreign ministers had pressed the EU executive to draw up a series of possible options to curb trade coming from settlements in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ meeting in Brussels on Monday . <p> The EU could " introduce a partial or total prohibition on the import , transit , marketing , and distribution of any goods originating from the illegal settlements , " the paper said . <p> The commission 's options paper said the EU could use tariffs as another way to cut off trade with Israeli settlements . <p> " These tariffs could be set at prohibitively high levels , rendering the import of settlement goods economically unviable and effectively excluding them from the EU market , " noted the internal document . <p> The Republic , the Netherlands , and Belgium have separately pursued national legislation to ban trade coming from occupied Palestinian territories . <p> Brussels officials said the EU could alternatively introduce an " import licensing system " for goods originating in the illegal settlements , meaning companies would have to apply for a licence to export products into the EU market . <p> It is a long-standing EU position not to recognise Israel 's sovereignty over territories it occupied since 1967 in the West Bank , East Jerusalem , Gaza @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ settlements do not benefit from the terms of an EU-Israel free trade deal and instead are charged tariffs when imported into European countries . <p> The EU is Israel 's largest trading partner and accounts for 31 per cent of the country 's total trade in goods . <p> The amount of exports from illegal settlements in occupied territories is small , with some estimates suggesting they account for 0.5 per cent of total trade from Israel to the EU market . <p> The commission 's paper said it was likely there would be ways businesses in Israeli settlements could circumvent any trade ban or restrictions , by masking products sold to the EU as exports coming from Israel . <p> @@138820861 <p> The testimonies of 10 doctors -- most of them non-Palestinian volunteers working on short missions to Gaza during 2024 and 2025 -- form the backbone of this distressing and damning documentary , which is sober in execution and furious in spirit . Harrowing on-the-ground video diaries and reflective studio interviews offer a portrait of a noble and non-partisan profession battling cruelty and indifference while working in some of the worst conditions imaginable . Life Support argues , without reservation , that these doctors witnessed a genocide . The accusation is delivered calmly , methodically and with the weight of first-hand experience and observation . <p> Sober in execution and furious in spirit <p> The documentary repeatedly makes the point that these doctors are the eyes of the world . ' We are the vessel of their stories , ' says British surgeon Ana Jeelani , talking of Gaza 's two-million residents , who lived through a sustained Israeli military offensive after the Hamas attack on Israel in October 2023 . To be a visiting doctor in a territory where independent observers and international journalists are barred from entering adds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The first-hand testimonies collected by director Daniele Rugo , a UK-based Italian academic and documentarian ( The Soil And The Sea , About A War ) -- who could not himself enter Gaza -- make Life Support a significant addition to the reporting on the war . The film is released in the UK and Ireland on July 10 after a world premiere at Sheffield DocFest ; specialist audiences elsewhere should also welcome the rare window it opens on the conflict . <p> Life Support does not get overly bogged down in the mechanics of how and why these doctors are present in Gaza , interesting though that is . Through their moving and eloquent accounts of their work we learn that they are usually on weeks- or months-long missions on behalf of NGOs such as Medical Aid For Palestinians . It 's also clear -- especially from the recollections of repeat visits across 2024 and 2025 by British surgeons Victoria Rose and Nick Maynard , among others -- that their ability to enter Gaza and bring supplies with them was progressively curtailed . ( Baby milk powder was routinely @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of war , as seen through these doctors ' eyes , is the film 's chief interest . We hear -- and see , sometimes in sickening detail -- the effect of weapons blasts on children and adults . We see a hospital ward in bits after an explosion ; a mass grave being excavated at Al-Shifa Hospital after it was ' more or less levelled ' in April 2024 . It 's a list that could go on and on . But British A&amp;E doctor James Smith sums it up when he says what he has witnessed in Gaza " are the most horrific things I 've seen as an emergency doctor " . <p> There is a thoughtful balance between portraying the experiences ( and , sometimes , the emotions , although the medical restraint is palpable ) of these foreign doctors at work and reflecting the parallel experiences of their Gazan colleagues and patients . There 's a reminder , too , that medical care in a war zone does n't just extend to those injured by conflict . One doctor argues that cancer patients being denied @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ torture ' . <p> In the absence of journalists and other observers , these doctors find themselves answering questions beyond their usual remit and expertise . The consensus among the film 's interviewees is that they are working amid a co-ordinated genocide or ' ethnic cleansing ' ( different medics land on different terms ) ; we see Canadian doctor Tanya Haj-Hassan testifying as such to the U.N . <p> The filmmakers avoid dwelling on what must have been a difficult production process ; we 're left to imagine the challenges of making video diaries in these circumstances and of finding a narrative among the chaos of war . ( As a viewer , it 's difficult enough to follow the timeline of the hospitals attacked , reconstructed , reopened and again attacked . ) Some knowledge of the wider conflict and political context is assumed ; this is a film designed to build on general reporting , not repeat it . A mournful quiet score by Massive Attack 's Robert Del Naja and others , plus a voiceover reading by Irish actress Denise Gough of excerpts of the 2025 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ subtle poetic additions to a film in which the gathered recollections and imagery are powerful enough alone to provoke anger and sorrow . <h> Subscribe to Screen International <p> Screen International is the essential resource for the international film industry . @@138820995 <p> A " breakthrough " has been made in the investigation into crimes committed during Sudan 's civil war in the Darfur region allowing prosecutors to link them to leadership , a senior International Criminal Court official said . <p> The ICC is investigating attacks on the cities of Al Geneina , in 2023 , and Al Fasher last year , where UN experts say forces from the paramilitary Rapid Support Forces committed crimes that bear the " hallmarks of genocide " against people from non-Arab tribes . <p> " We have got additional evidence , strong evidence , linking what is occurring in Darfur with leadership levels . And we are very , very pleased to say that this is a breakthrough for us , " deputy prosecutor Nazhat Shameem Khan told Reuters , following a visit to eastern Chad to meet victims of the attacks . <p> She did not specify the forces the leadership belong to and could not , according to ICC rules , say whether warrants had been or would be applied for . <p> " We are confident that there are going to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ added , without giving a timeframe . <p> In international war crimes trials of political leaders it is often hard to link them to specific atrocity crimes committed by lower level perpetrators . Prosecutors need so-called linkage evidence -- often in the form of insider witnesses or physical records -- of political leadership being briefed about operations and plans on the ground . <p> Al Geneina and Al Fasher saw the most intense violence in the civil war between the Sudanese army and the RSF that continued for more than three years . The RSF now controls both cities , and Khan told the UN Security Council in January that the paramilitary group had not cooperated with investigations . <p> The RSF has said it did not target civilians in the attacks and would hold individual perpetrators accountable . <p> A Reuters documentary on the Al Fasher takeover identified several RSF leaders committing or in the vicinity of attacks through interviews and analysis of videos posted online . <p> Khan said the ICC probes included similar testimonies collected by ICC investigators . <p> Khan said witnesses spoke of executions and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ also told in the course of our proceedings , " she said . <p> Sudan is not a party to the Rome Statute , and therefore not a member of the ICC , but the UN Security Council gave the court jurisdiction over atrocity crimes committed in Darfur from 2005 onwards . The country 's army-led government has cooperated with investigations on the most recent attacks , but has not handed over several top former leaders accused of genocide and other attacks in the earlier conflict . <p> No public warrants have yet been issued during the current war , which began in April 2023 . <p> Three countries in West Africa 's Sahel region -- Niger , Mali and Burkina Faso -- announced last year that they would withdraw from the Rome Statute , and the court said on July 1 that they had submitted letters initiating the process , which takes a year . <p> " I hope they change their minds because I see a great virtue in being part of the Rome Statute family . @@138851215 <h> Irish people are 13th happiest in the world yet loneliest in the EU . It does n't add up <h> We should do what the Finns do : give ourselves a pat on the back for all that is good in this country of ours <p> Sunbathing in Rosscarbery , Co Cork : this country has reasons to be cheerful . Photograph : Andy Gibson <p> We 're not in the World Cup , but we Irish are happy out . Scotland 's football fans have stolen Boston 's old Irish heart from us with their bagpipes and ballads about wee birdies and the bloomin ' heather , but Ireland just keeps smiling through . <p> Hard to believe if you 're a fan of the D ? il 's Leaders ' Questions or RT ? Radio 1 's Liveline whine-line , but ours is the second-happiest English-speaking country in the world . Only New Zealand is more content in the anglophone sphere ; an advantage , surely , of being cast adrift from the rest of the noisy planet . <p> The World Happiness Report for 2026 places @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 10 . Compared to happiest-of-all Finland and its 7.74 score , we 're not exactly delirious , but we 've climbed from 15th place last year and from 17th in 2024 , suggesting we 're getting ever more s ? sta . As the rankings use respondents ' subjective assessments of their own country 's state of contentment , Ireland -- champion moaner and ologoaner -- might even be happier than it realises . <p> Self-assessment can be an unreliable measure . If the report 's compilers -- the University of Oxford 's Wellbeing Research Centre , polling company Gallup and the UN Sustainable Development Solutions Network -- gave marks for positivity , Israel would win . Despite waging wars on several fronts and living under an Iron Dome , it is eighth in the 2026 list and was fifth in 2024 , the first full year of Israel 's slaughter of Palestinians in Gaza . <p> The happy league does not rely solely on self-assessment , however . It also takes account of economics , psychology and sociology . If we are to believe the rankings , Ireland is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ health services ; homelessness ; traffic jams ; crazy prices of houses , coffee and insurance ; increasing numbers of women being murdered ; and children facing parlously long waits for spinal surgery . <p> While " greatest " lists are best consumed with a grain of salt , they should not be dismissed . We can learn from considering what makes the Finns , stashed up there under Earth 's dark and icy roof , so blissed out . It ca n't be all that sweating in saunas and rolling starkers in the snow . Once you 've seen the aurora borealis , you 've been there , done that , bought the T-shirt and , anyway , the Irish winter sky has green and blue streaks , too . <p> The Finns drink more alcohol than we do and reside next door to nuclear enemy Russia . It 's dark for so long even Santa Claus makes a mad dash out of the place every Christmas . The 2026 World Population Review , a privately operated demographics monitor , shows Finland 's suicide rate ( 15.88 per 100,000 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ according to UN Population Fund data for this year , life expectancy for men there is a year shorter than Irish men 's 81 years . <p> So are the Finns utterly deluded in believing themselves to be the happiest people on earth ? It must make them even happier to think they are and , yes , the metrics of any greatest-this-or-that list can be disputed , but it 's no accident the Nordic country has been topping the annual World Happiness list since 2018 . <p> Finland has reasons to be cheerful . Its people pay high taxes , but in return they get an enviable , heavily subsidised childcare system , universal free healthcare , low levels of crime and a reliable public transport service . Ireland , on the other hand , is stressed out from healthcare queues , increasing numbers of violent assaults on women ( according to crime statistics for the first quarter of 2026 ) , unaffordable housing , profligate public spending on white elephant projects and the apoplexy-inducing phenomenon of the ghost bus that now is coming , now it 's not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ despite its climate , a lifestyle immersed in nature , but the contrast with Ireland is about more than picnicking in the woods . It 's a thing called social capital . Remember Bertie Ahern had a stab at it when he was taoiseach . He invited Robert Putnam , the US academic who wrote Bowling Alone , to address Fianna F ? il 's parliamentary party think-in more than 20 years ago . Putnam , whom Ahern hailed as " an extraordinary genius " , promoted the theory that countries with active civic participation and high levels of trust fare better than those impelled by individualism and monetary wealth . <p> He was preaching to the converted here . The spirit of meitheal springing out from co-ops , parish networks and voluntary charitable and sports organisations was flourishing . Since then , the Catholic church has lost its dominion , the country 's population is on the verge of doubling and urbanisation has created chronic time-poverty that eats away at those social networks . <p> A European Commission survey found Ireland had the highest level of loneliness in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ felt lonely most or all of the time ; a whopping gap with the 13 per cent recorded for all Europeans polled . It is an ironic twist in the Irish psyche that the more the economy depends on tax revenue from big tech , the more social media use compounds discontent . Studies have found a distinct link between internet activity and lower life satisfaction . <p> Still , this country has reasons to be cheerful . In yet another global list , Ireland lies second in The Good Country Index , published by the World Population Review , for its contribution to " peace and world order " . Plus , we 're seventh in the 2026 Global Passport Index . <p> Social capital is about shared values and togetherness . There are elements , both among us and stretching from the White House to Silicon Valley , trying to divide us by whipping up negativity and loathing . We will let them win at our peril . <p> Finland prides itself on its enjoyment of nature and the simple pleasures , but there are more ways to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's good about our country is one . We should do what the Finns do and give ourselves a clap on the back for all that is good in this country of ours . 
@@138851216 <h> The big questions we 're busy ignoring about what it means to be European <h> EU presidency a chance for Ireland to depart from vacuous scripts on underpinning the bloc 's ' shared values ' <p> Justin Kelly , Garda Commissioner , and Chief of Staff of the Defence Forces Lieut Gen Rossa Mulcahy at the ceremony to mark the start of Ireland 's EU presidency . Photograph : Alan Betson <p> Garret FitzGerald , who served as minister for foreign affairs between 1973 and 1977 , was acommitted Europhile . His tenure as minister coincided with Ireland 's first presidency of the Council of the European Union , then the nine-member European Economic Community ( EEC ) . FitzGerald 's parents were involved in the 1916 Rising , but he did not see Ireland 's embrace of the EEC as a betrayal of their fight ; as early as 1962 , after Ireland first sought to join the EEC , he insisted : " The voice of Ireland will be heard in Europe in the decades ahead . But for the sacrifices of those who won our freedom @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ EEC membership would lead , he hoped , to a discarding of economic dependence on Britain , for which some diminution of sovereignty was an acceptable price . But that same year , secretary of the Department of Finance , TK Whitaker , in correspondence with Fianna F ? il minister James Ryan , referred to the need for a realistic acceptance of Ireland 's status : " Nobody so loves us as to want us in the EEC on our own terms . " Taoiseach Se ? n Lemass also doused Irish piety about neutrality with cold water that year , telling the D ? il Ireland would be prepared to yield " even the technical label of neutrality ... we are prepared to go into this integrated Europe without any reservation as to how far this will take us in the field of foreign policy and defence " . <p> Ten years later , with Ireland on the cusp of membership , it was stated explicitly by the Department of Foreign Affairs ( DFA ) that the Republic was " a small country with little capacity at present @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ In 1976 , an internal memorandum in the DFA noted " Ireland 's commitment to European integration springs from a combination of idealism and enlightened self-interest " . Historian John A Murphy suggested the same year that in Ireland " there was little feeling of being European " . Irish economist David O'Mahony said : " It is deplorable that the subject of defence is not discussed openly . " <p> Twenty years later Ireland 's first White Paper on foreign policy was published and it included the dubious declaration " we see ourselves , increasingly , as European " . Minister for foreign affairs at that time , Dick Spring , speaking about the White Paper , insisted " a foreign policy that is not based on a sense of values would be sterile and amoral " . <p> It is worth considering these historic assertions at the outset of Ireland 's current presidency of the Council of the EU . The Government is making much of the broad themes it has chosen for its presidency : competitiveness , security and values , amounting to , we are told @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , equality , the rule of law and respect for human rights ... and they underpin our competitiveness and our security " . <p> It is a grab-bag of ideals , but raises various questions : Is Ireland in favour of more security and defence integration and spending ? What would that mean for us ? What does neutrality mean now ? Where does Ireland stand on how the EU approaches relations with Israel , Russia , the US ; on climate change and migration ? <p> Is it true , in the words of former president Michael D Higgins , that the EU has witnessed a " massively increased realm of the unaccountable " alongside a " hegemonic theory of the market " causing displacement , hostility to regulation and " degradation of the environment " , amounting to a display of " hubris , of uncritical pursuit of ever-accelerating growth " ? <p> National Archive files relating to the first presidency in 1975 underline concern about being seen " as an efficient , businesslike administration and country " . Other priorities then were to influence regional and social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , " get us up to the starting line " . There was also worry about enlargement as it " would be directly detrimental to the economic and financial interest of Ireland " . Another concern was to " avoid , if possible , a commitment to co-operation in arms production and to be hesitant about regular exchanges of views on defence/security matters " . <p> Preoccupation with the economics of membership persisted for decades . Ireland 's net receipts from the EU , in absolute terms , peaked at ? 2.5 billion in 1997 . Ireland remained a net beneficiary of the EU budget until 2006 and first became a net contributor in 2013 . <p> But much else has happened in parallel , including expansion and migration -- in 2007 , about 85,000 people came to Ireland from the new EU member states -- multiple referendums , further integration , economic meltdown , Brexit , Trumpism and the wars on Ukraine and Gaza . <p> Ireland supports further enlargement , yet generally sticks to vacuous scripts about underpinning the EU 's " shared values " , with little @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? What does " being European " mean ? What is the " voice of Ireland " and how is it morally framed ? <p> It would be refreshing if the presidency were accompanied by some interrogation of these questions instead of a preoccupation with external validation.

@@138880099 <p> The facts of his case should be repeated plainly , because repetition is sometimes all that stands between outrage and disappearance . <p> Abu Safiya was imprisoned without charge by Israeli forces on December 27 , 2024 , after a raid on Kamal Adwan Hospital . <p> On the same day , his 15-year-old son Ibrahim was killed in a drone strike on the hospital 's entrance . The facility had been one of the last functioning medical facilities in northern Gaza . <p> He had stayed there through siege , bombardment , and collapse , continuing to treat patients in conditions no doctor should ever have to describe , never mind endure . <p> Of course , Israel has accused him of links to Hamas . Those allegations have not been tested in open court and no charges have been brought . No public evidence has ever been produced . <p> His captivity has instead continued under Israel 's " unlawful combatants " law , a mechanism which allows people -- many of them children -- to be held without the ordinary protections of due process . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for his release , reportedly relying on confidential material not shared with either Abu Safiya or his lawyer . <p> This week , the case became still more urgent . His lawyer , Nasser Odeh , who visited him in custody , said Abu Safiya was in critical condition . Physicians for Human Rights Israel reported visible injuries , severe deterioration , difficulty breathing , and repeated loss of consciousness . <p> Amnesty International has warned that his life is in grave danger . The UN Working Group on Arbitrary Detention has called for his immediate release . <p> An image of Dr. Hussam Abu Safiya , director of Kamal Adwan Hospital taken before his arrest at Kamal Adwan Hospital , walking towards the Israeli Army tanks in his lab coat . <p> Israel 's prison authorities deny mistreatment . But denial is not due process . Denial is not a medical examination . Denial is not a charge sheet . Denial is not an answer to 561 days ' imprisonment . <p> There is , of course , a danger in making Abu Safiya 's case sound exceptional . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but not in its moral architecture . He is one of many Palestinian healthcare workers detained , killed , displaced , starved , exhausted , or forced to work inside the ruins of hospitals that should have been protected under every humanitarian principle we claim to value . <p> What more must a person be ? <p> Still , perhaps we should linger on his profession for a moment . Not because a doctor 's life is worth more than anyone else 's . Not because Palestinians must first be useful , educated , gentle , professional , or recognisable to western eyes before their suffering becomes legible . That trap is part of the problem . <p> But even on the narrowest terms available to western conscience , what more must a person be ? <p> Abu Safiya is a medical doctor . He ran a hospital . He treated children . He stayed with patients when leaving might have saved him . He belonged to that profession we applauded from windows during covid , praised as heroic , described as sacred , invoked whenever we wanted to remind @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ yet where is the great professional revolt ? Where are the hospital corridors lined in protest ? Where are the medical colleges , the ministers , the university presidents , the trade unions , the Irish political class speaking with the urgency this case demands ? <p> Yes , there have been very worthy protests . The collective Irish Healthcare Workers for Palestine have been among the most vocal and consistent solidarity groups on this island . <p> But that is precisely the indictment . The moral labour has again been outsourced to ordinary people . <p> A Palestinian doctor can be held without charge for more than a year and a half , reportedly tortured , reportedly denied proper care , reportedly reduced to a condition where his lawyer fears he may die , and still the machinery of western politics barely shudders . <p> This is not a call for performative purity . It is not written from some imagined place of moral cleanliness . <p> Most of us are compromised by distance , exhaustion , helplessness , fear of saying the wrong thing , fear of being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a point at which helplessness becomes a habit . There is a point at which complexity becomes cover . There is a point at which " what can we do ? " becomes less a question than an alibi . <p> In Ireland , we know Palestinian doctors . They work in our hospitals . They live in our towns . They treat our parents , our children , our neighbours . <p> Abu Safiya is their peer . He is the peer of every consultant , registrar , nurse , paramedic , and healthcare assistant in the country . The fact that he is Palestinian should not make his suffering easier to file away . It should make our silence harder to bear . <p> Because this is not only about one man , though one man would be enough . <p> It is about whether the language we use about human rights , medical neutrality , and international law has any meaning when tested against power . It is about whether a hospital director can vanish into indefinite detention and the world can still congratulate itself for being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Failing that , he should be charged or freed , examined independently , given proper medical care , allowed full legal access and protected from abuse . <p> That is not radical . It is not inflammatory . It is the bare minimum . <p> And if the bare minimum now sounds extreme , that tells us less about Gaza than it does about ourselves .

@@138926164 <p> For Tareq Baconi , the personal became the political when what began as a book about his first love turned into a memoir exploring his heritage as the grandchild of Palestinian refugees . The writer , scholar and activist grew up in Jordan after his family was expelled first from Palestine , then Lebanon during the civil war ; he is now based in London . <p> The displacement he experienced was more than geographical ; it was only when he moved away from home that he also felt able to come to terms with his identity as a queer man . <p> Baconi 's book , Fire in Every Direction : A Memoir , which he will discuss at the upcoming West Cork Literary Festival , begins with him unpacking a box of letters from a childhood friend named Ramzi . <p> " The memoir came about accidentally . I wanted to write a book about the friendship between Ramzi and I. It was a friendship that was very important to me , and a friendship that developed into love , from my end at least . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and I could n't articulate to anyone , including him , despite my best efforts . This book was initially written as a letter to Ramzi , trying to explain all of those years , and what they meant to me . <p> " But very quickly over the course of the writing , the text revealed itself to me , and certain questions started emerging , including , why are these two boys in Jordan -- they 're both the descendants of Palestinian refugees , their homes are Palestinian home -- and so political questions started intruding into the story , " he says . <p> To be allowed the right to live in Jordan , Baconi 's mother had to renounce all political activity , a restriction that reverberated throughout their lives . <p> " In the book , I trace her rage and diminishment to that moment , where she is told that to have a life and become a mother , she had to be essentially apolitical . So I grew up , as did most of my contemporaries , in a situation where I @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wanted a certain safety or security , that one needed to be relatively benign . I do n't think I understood that consciously , it was just in the air around me . " It was also an imposed silence that was echoed in the secrecy and shame around his sexuality and falling in love with Ramzi . <p> " I did n't quite understand what was happening . I write about the confusion around the fact that this could n't possibly be love because obviously love is between a man and a woman . It was a love that was very much grounded in shame , that this is n't right , that it is sinful and something that must be resisted . I really did n't think about queerness in that form until I left Jordan , it was completely absent from the world around me . There were no narratives or symbols or signs of homosexuality . " <p> Fire In Every Direction , by Tareq Baconi . <p> Baconi says that while writing the book was cathartic in some ways , it was also a confronting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was writing it that it felt visceral still . It was n't lost on me that there is something masochistic in going to this place that , at least in my childhood , I thought was shameful and then owning it publicly . It is almost like I am wearing my shame for everyone to see . But that is where power lies for me . I can say , this has no longer has power over me , I have reclaimed it completely . " <p> Coming out was difficult for Baconi , and his father in particular was not receptive -- he describes how his mother put sedatives in his father 's coffee to dampen any confrontation . However , his father , who has since passed away , eventually came to terms with his sexuality , embracing Baconi 's husband as his son-in-law . <p> " The fact that my father was able to move from quite stubborn rejection to a place of not just acceptance but love is something that I do n't take for granted . I feel very grateful that he was able to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , he had to do the work and I had to do the work with him , " says Baconi . <p> He describes the reaction from the queer community , in Palestine and beyond , as one of the " greatest gifts " of the book , with many people reaching out to tell him how the book has resonated with them . He says life for queer people in Palestine in particular is especially difficult given the ongoing conflict there . <p> " If you are living in historic Palestine , there are really two things you 're dealing with , the more important and the more profound of that is State violence and the colonial regime and how it 's shaping your life , " he says . <p> Baconi struggles with going about his daily life in London while the war in Gaza continues and Palestinians live under Israeli occupation . " I carry a lot of grief and rage . Even if on the surface one might be going on with life as normal , there is a lot of pain . I have been @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mom 's experience . The title Fire in Every Direction is about how my mom 's rage was burning in every direction in unpredictable ways . That resonates a lot with how I feel today . There is a rage that ca n't be harnessed and that is unpredictable and frightening . " <p> West Cork Literary Festival <p> Baconi is looking forward to discovering West Cork on his visit to Ireland , and says he greatly appreciates the solidarity shown to the Palestinian people here . <p> " It is incredible , I really do n't have the words to describe how powerful it is and how grateful Palestinians are for it . When we see the Irish people taking it on as their struggle as well , it is hugely affirming . @@138926337 <p> The far-right leader 's abuse of veto powers to continually block efforts to aid Ukraine pushed everybody else to search for creative ways to settle important debates by majority vote , rather than unanimity , which is the standard ( high ) bar to clear for EU decisions on foreign policy . <p> Now Orb ? n is gone and the commission has suddenly become a fan of unanimous decision-making , at least when it concerns sanctioning Israel . <p> An attempt to ban trade between the EU and illegal Israeli settlements in occupied Palestinian territories has become bogged down in a legally ambiguous debate about procedure . Is it a foreign policy decision , or a trade one ? The answer matters . <p> To approve a change in trade policy Brussels only requires a " qualified majority " , which means the support of at least 15 member states , who together make up about two thirds of the bloc 's population . The Czech government or another country fiercely protective of Israel would be sure to block a proposal that requires unanimous EU approval . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ illegal settlements is to ground it as a trade measure . <p> However , the commission has argued the ban aims to achieve a " foreign policy objective " by sending a political message to Israel . That has been read as the Berlaymont signalling any proposed ban it puts on the table would need the backing of all 27 member states . <p> The argument looks to be a fairly shaky one . <p> European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen is on record as supporting the EU taking more foreign policy decisions by way of qualified majority , rather than unanimity . When the commission last year proposed suspending an EU-Israel free trade deal to protest against Israel 's military campaign in Gaza , it repeatedly said only a qualified majority was required . <p> That proposal never received sufficient support from member states . Italy had been wavering and many believed Rome was on the verge of swinging the debate in favour of suspending Israel 's preferential trading arrangement , but a peace deal in Gaza killed off momentum to sanction Israel . <p> A trade ban @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ easier sell to capitals hesitant to damage their relations with Israel proper . It seems there would be a majority , or very close to one , to ban trade coming from settlements . <p> The commission 's suggestion that the bar suddenly be raised to require the support of all 27 governments seems to be a pretty clumsy effort to steer the discussion into a debate with no end and then shrug its shoulders when there 's no agreement .

But while Brussels continues to mobilize billions of euros , its financial leverage has n't been matched by the political pressure needed to tackle the conflict 's underlying causes . <p> At Monday'sPalestine Donor Group meeting in Brussels , the European Commission announced nearly ? 900 million in funding commitments and launched the Team Gaza Initiative , a platform bringing together international partners to coordinate the Strip 's early socioeconomic recovery . " A more effective approach would actually be for the EU , its member states , and other actors to mobilize more aggressively against these Israeli sanctions . " <p> After a decade out of the spotlight , Jimmy MacCarthy is back -- with a run of sold-out shows in October , and plans to re-release two of his classic ' 90s albums . The 73-year-old songwriter is , in fact , 30 minutes early for our interview -- but he 's right on the second count too . After more than half-a-century immersed in Irish music , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ album , The Song of the Singing Horseman , he is here to finally tell his own story , on his own terms . <p> It 's been a long time coming for fans too . " But over 40 years ... That 's a long time to stay quiet , is n't it ? Wicklow . <p> Advertisement <p> There are aspects of his personal life -- and his unique struggles -- about which he can not speak , as events take their circuitous route towards a kind of completion . They struggled , but I learned that they were more than I thought they were -- because they were the same , and better , than they were when they had everything .
